# Claim ledger

Source claims retain their IDs and witnesses. Sounds, meanings and readings are Bonta’s proposals. PDF-review corrections separate lexical construction, source extraction and object identity; earlier ledger states remain in history/.

## Q001

**method**. Source: B24, Sections III–IV, PDF pp. 34–36, 55 and 77–79.

He proposes a partial logosyllabic decipherment of an early Indo-Aryan language.

Evidence: He proposes Indo-Aryan in segment 54 and CV/root signs with logograms in segments 76–78; segment 33 discusses a logosyllabic inventory.

Tests: D1, D2, L1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q002

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L01, 0:02:54–0:05:20.

He describes a Cornell linguistics PhD and earlier work on Dravidian and Indo-European languages.

Evidence: Autobiographical account, also in the introductory letter p. 6.

Tests: . Status: Exploratory.

## Q003

**segmentation or structure**. Source: L03, 0:06:26–0:08:34; B24 segment 15.

Most inscriptions are read right to left; seals and impressions require distinguishing the mirrored face.

Evidence: A seal and its mirrored impression are compared; he uses the impression as the canonical right-to-left display. The grounds for direction are left largely unstated.

Tests: D1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q004

**segmentation or structure**. Source: B24, Section III, PDF pp. 16–34.

Patterned texts have recurring ordered fields; complex texts contain more variable, language-like sequences.

Evidence: Repeated sign classes, junctures, substitutions and positional restrictions.

Tests: D1, D2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q005

**method**. Source: L03, 0:56:37–0:59:59.

He distinguishes transcriptive writing from notational writing and treats both as present in Indus texts.

Evidence: English weights, prices, dates and mathematical formulas illustrate abbreviation, changed spoken order and mixed notation. He announces application to Harappan texts at 0:59:44.

Tests: D2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q006

**segmentation or structure**. Source: L04, 0:36:28–0:39:58; B24 segments 16–20.

Reading-order fields are P, M, C and T, with optional omissions. P is a position label rather than a grammatical prefix.

Evidence: Illustrated sequences show P–M–C–T and missing fields; P is explained as a positional convenience at 0:39:16.

Tests: D2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q007

**corpus-distributional**. Source: L04, 0:02:10–0:10:38; B24 segment 20.

His M-field model has three optional components: an oval subfield, a fish subfield and an MS pair of a container with a central stick beside three long bars.

Evidence: Illustrated recurrent clusters.

Tests: D2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q008

**segmentation or structure**. Source: L04, 0:09:55–0:10:17; B24 segment 20.

Within a complete M field, the reading order is oval, fish, MS.

Evidence: Repeated complete fields; manuscript explicitly reverses its printed left-to-right display.

Tests: D2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q009

**corpus-distributional**. Source: L04, 0:06:00–0:09:35; B24 segments 18–19.

The fish subfield uses seven entries, has at most three entries and does not repeat an entry.

Evidence: Selected sequences and a proposed compound-sign treatment of fish with paired strokes.

Tests: D2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q010

**corpus-distributional**. Source: B24, Section III, PDF pp. 21; L06 0:01:27–0:02:31.

The oval subfield has at most two non-repeating members of a three-sign inventory.

Evidence: The textual definition gives the inventory size and maximum count; the lecture supplies non-repetition. The available manuscript text omits the glyphs.

Tests: D2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q011

**corpus-distributional**. Source: B24, Section III, PDF pp. 21and PDF pp. 51–54; L04 0:03:34–0:04:15.

He treats the container-with-stick and three-long-bars pairing as an MS construction, usually concluding an M field.

Evidence: The recurrent pair is defined in segment 20 and illustrated in Table 11; segments 52–53 propose mātra from its placement and the suggested tra value.

Tests: D2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q012

**corpus-distributional**. Source: B24, Section III, PDF pp. 15; PDF pp. 56–61.

Jar and arrow signs function as terminal-field signs and can accompany other fields.

Evidence: Segment 14 describes final or penultimate placement, including ends of embedded sequences; segments 55–60 argue for predicates from recurring terminal contexts.

Tests: D1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q013

**segmentation or structure**. Source: L09, 0:04:17–0:13:31.

He doubts that the juncture signs are case endings and tentatively suggests honorific or benefactive functions; gender and number are additional possibilities.

Evidence: He compares juncture combinations with P signs and reports exceptions to their mostly contrasting distributions. At 0:09:42 he doubts case endings; at 0:10:04 he labels his alternative tentative.

Tests: D1, D3. Status: Exploratory.

Source wording: “at a very very tentative guess”

## Q014

**method**. Source: L10, 0:05:00–0:06:27.

He clarifies that his CASE label represents a case-like relationship, perhaps dative/benefactive or genitive.

Evidence: Context of proposed patterned readings; exact relationship remains tentative.

Tests: D3. Status: Exploratory.

## Q015

**method**. Source: B24, Section III, PDF pp. 35–36.

He compared initial, medial and final CV-syllable frequencies in Monier-Williams and the Cologne Tamil lexicon with deduplicated Indus sign dossiers. Both comparisons largely failed, apart from a handful of signs.

Evidence: He describes substring-frequency queries and dossiers for signs with roughly more than 20 occurrences; he attributes the failure partly to stylized inscriptions.

Tests: . Status: Exploratory.

## Q016

**typology**. Source: L05, 0:10:25–0:15:04; B24 Table 6, segments 38–40.

Recurring shorter C-field sequences are concatenated in a longer C field, which he interprets as evidence for noun compounds.

Evidence: Table 6 compares autonomous C sequences with their combination, especially MH4335; the passage supplies no reordering or suffix contrast.

Tests: D3. Status: Exploratory.

## Q017

**typology**. Source: B24, Table 6–7 discussion, PDF pp. 42.

The manuscript treats noun compounding as relatively rare in Dravidian and much more extensive in Sanskrit.

Evidence: A comparison with Sanskrit and Dravidian examples.

Tests: D3. Status: Exploratory.

## Q018

**typology**. Source: L05, 0:28:36–0:30:01; B24 segment 41.

He categorically says attested Dravidian lacks noun compounding, while allowing that a Harappan-period ancestor could have had it. The paper makes a weaker comparison about extensive multi-noun name compounds.

Evidence: He compares inferred compounding, gemination and prefixing with Indo-Aryan; the spoken hedge allows earlier typological change. Krishnamurti’s chapter summary documents noun-plus-noun compounds.

Tests: D3. Status: Exploratory.

Source wording: “do not allow noun compounding in any form”

## Q019

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: B24, Section III, PDF pp. 43–55.

He identifies Indo-Aryan as the most likely language from inferred compounding, affix behavior and proposed lexical readings.

Evidence: Selected typological observations and Sanskrit matches.

Tests: D3, L1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q020

**method**. Source: L06, 0:01:27–0:04:58; B24 segments 20–24.

He uses M fields as an entering wedge because metrological referents could constrain candidate values.

Evidence: The lecture summarizes limited non-repeating fish/oval combinations and the invariant MS pair. Segment 23 separately compares the inventory sizes with Marshall’s weight series.

Tests: M1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q021

**sign value (phonetic or logographic)**. Source: B24, Metrological discussion, PDF pp. 24–25.

He rates it very likely that seven fish signs correspond to Marshall’s 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64 series and three oval signs to 160, 320, 640.

Evidence: Matching inventory sizes and the absence of geminated fish signs motivate the hypothesis; he gives no sign-by-sign assignment to weighed objects.

Tests: M1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q022

**sign value (phonetic or logographic)**. Source: L06, 0:08:52–0:13:53; B24 segments 47–50, 66–67.

He proposes pala for the staff/fork-like sign, using a Sanskrit straw/weight association and its non-M uses.

Evidence: Grain-staff graphology, numeral adjacency and the Sanskrit pala weight motivate the first value; at 0:12:03–0:13:53 he adds pāla, protector, for non-metrological contexts.

Tests: L1, M1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q023

**sign value (phonetic or logographic)**. Source: L06, 0:14:37–0:18:48; B24 segments 52–53.

He reads the MS construction as ma-tra and proposes matra, a measure or amount.

Evidence: The recurring two-part construction and proposed word-final tra behavior.

Tests: L1, D1, D2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q024

**sign value (phonetic or logographic)**. Source: L06, 0:18:48–0:24:42; B24 segments 44–46, 54.

He proposes dhana/dhanu-related values for the bow-bearing figure, linking bow and wealth.

Evidence: At 0:19:30–0:21:58 he compares bow and bowman substitutions and asset-like contexts; the following Sanskrit bow/wealth comparison motivates near-related values.

Tests: L1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q025

**method**. Source: L07, 0:05:28–0:11:55; B24 segments 69–70.

He obtains the proposed PU/bhu value by intersecting words ending in dhana, ma and pala, then requiring that the shared initial element also be common as a final or suffix element.

Evidence: Three Cologne dictionary substring lists supply candidate X values; the fourth condition is final/suffix use. The same contexts helped choose PU and later appear in the tested panel.

Tests: L2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q026

**method**. Source: L07, 0:18:32–0:24:37; B24 forewords, segments 2–7.

He reports developing values on Mahadevan, then checking them on ICIT material with approximately 12–15 readable new inscriptions and one correction.

Evidence: His research-history account reports a dozen or fifteen readable inscriptions and one value withdrawn. The caption permits both no unreadable attempts and no readable counterexample; no attempted list resolves the ambiguity.

Tests: V1. Status: Exploratory.

Source wording: “nothing that was not readable, nothing that that contradicted my results”

## Q027

**method**. Source: B24, Catalogue conventions, PDF pp. 85.

An ICIT-first label generally means it was first encountered there, but can instead signal a more accurate transcription.

Evidence: Explicit source-labelling convention.

Tests: V1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q028

**method**. Source: B24, Cross-checking, PDF pp. 67–78.

He strengthens proposed values by repeated lexical cross-checking in different contexts, yielding recognizable Sanskrit names, titles and compounds.

Evidence: PU and PATI are chosen by dictionary searches across their contexts; segment 76 describes trial and error and abandoned candidates.

Tests: L1, L2. Status: Exploratory.

## Q029

**method**. Source: B24, Section IV introduction and Table 18, PDF pp. 78–82.

He lists 55 selected signs plus additional logographic/ligature constructions and leaves many frequent and rare signs unresolved.

Evidence: He states 55 signs in segment 77; Table 18 adds certain logograms and ligatures. He also identifies omitted numerical compounds and unresolved signs.

Tests: L1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q030

**reading of a text**. Source: B24, Section IV, PDF pp. 83–217.

He offers a long catalogue of complete or partial readings as names, titles and compounds, arranged under individual proposed sign values.

Evidence: Segment 82 introduces the catalogue and segments 83–84 explain source labels, alternate readings and the format. The package’s occurrence parser supplies a separate count.

Tests: L1, V1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q031

**method**. Source: L08, 0:59:59–1:04:00; B24 segment 81.

Some compound signs are interpreted as ligatures with honorific or lexical values; ordering alternatives may be admitted.

Evidence: Proposed graphic compositions and readings such as shripati and related forms.

Tests: L1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q032

**sign value (phonetic or logographic)**. Source: B24, Segments 55–65; Table 18, PDF pp. 82.

He proposes AS for the jar, ISH for the arrow and ASTI for a group of asset-related signs.

Evidence: He argues for AS from terminal distributions and possessive copula analogies, ISH from valuation and arrow homophony, and ASTI from modified-jar distributions and property vocabulary.

Tests: D1, M1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q033

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L09, 0:36:37–0:37:42.

He links the proposed ASTI readings to property/asset vocabulary in Sanskrit and later languages.

Evidence: Lexical analogy and patterned contexts.

Tests: M1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q034

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L10, 0:01:07–0:03:36.

He calls the short seal examples sufficient to show asset or metrological notations and says they cannot reasonably be interpreted otherwise.

Evidence: Numeral-like strokes beside a staff sign on seals with bosses and animal figures are his stated evidence; 0:03:13–0:03:36 gives the categorical interpretation.

Tests: D2, M1. Status: Exploratory.

Source wording: “necessary and sufficient”

## Q035

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L10, 0:04:00–0:16:48; B24 segments 241–243.

He proposes deposits, pledges and temple donations as uses of patterned inscriptions.

Evidence: His proposed names and amounts are joined to analogies with temples as depositories/lenders and later donative epigraphy; 0:16:28–0:16:48 describes both functions.

Tests: M1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q036

**reading of a text**. Source: L10, 0:16:48–0:18:10; 0:30:10–0:30:32.

He interprets MH4470 and MH4603 as oblation-related records, including proposed chu offerings.

Evidence: The first passage gives proposed readings of MH4470 and MH4603; 0:30:10 separately links chu to a rice/grain offering or its preparation pot.

Tests: M1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q037

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L10, 0:28:25–0:31:14.

He infers princes, monarchs and caste-related titles from his name and title readings.

Evidence: Readings including bahuja and royal honorifics.

Tests: L1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q038

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L10, 0:30:32–0:31:14.

He suggests some lunar-dynasty mythology could preserve features of Indus society.

Evidence: A proposed Somabhu royal name and later mythic narratives.

Tests: . Status: Exploratory.

## Q039

**evaluation criterion**. Source: L10, 0:31:14–0:32:19.

He explicitly leaves common context-invariant and infrequent signs unresolved.

Evidence: Insufficient contextual variation to cross-check their values.

Tests: . Status: Exploratory.

## Q040

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L11, 0:00:22–0:03:57.

He describes the main language as early Indo-Aryan and leaves its relation to the Indo-Iranian split partly open.

Evidence: The proposed names and titles; no separate linguistic chronology test.

Tests: L1. Status: Exploratory.

## Q041

**typology**. Source: L11, 0:04:17–0:14:31.

He asserts that the language has prepositions beyond reasonable doubt, on his readings of ati, para, vi and related elements. He says the signs also occur elsewhere within words.

Evidence: Selected readings suggest prepositional values. At 0:11:00 he rejects strictly prefix-only status; at 0:14:10 he asserts the language-level conclusion.

Tests: D3. Status: Exploratory.

Source wording: “beyond any reasonable doubt”

## Q042

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L11, 0:19:26–0:24:00.

He proposes two possibilities for the suggested atima spelling: an approximate spelling of ātman or a phonetic rendering of an earlier form, with compensatory lengthening after vowel loss.

Evidence: He offers the earlier-form etymology tentatively at 0:21:35, describes the sound-change mechanism and leaves the judgement to the listener at 0:23:40.

Tests: . Status: Exploratory.

## Q043

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L11, 0:30:48–0:33:40.

He derives sabha from a proposed earlier sahapa through metathesis.

Evidence: A proposed inscription reading and Sanskrit saha/pa decomposition.

Tests: . Status: Exploratory.

## Q044

**typology**. Source: L11, 0:34:00–0:40:05.

He proposes a non-case ka affix with possessive/genitive-like uses and suggests it explains velar-related genitives in several modern Indo-Aryan languages.

Evidence: Selected readings such as Satyadevaka motivate possessive ka; later comparisons with Hindi, Sinhala, Marathi and Sindhi extend the claim to descent.

Tests: D3. Status: Exploratory.

## Q045

**evaluation criterion**. Source: L12, 0:04:17–0:04:40.

A claim of full decipherment should account for data that do not fit, including M-field distributions.

Evidence: Examples of competing claims ignoring patterned notational clusters.

Tests: C1-R. Status: Exploratory.

## Q046

**evaluation criterion**. Source: L12, 0:12:07–0:12:51.

Distributional evidence should determine script typology before assumptions about links to another script.

Evidence: Criticism of Brahmi-related proto-abugida proposals.

Tests: C1-R. Status: Exploratory.

## Q047

**evaluation criterion**. Source: L12, 0:28:36–0:28:57.

Proposed sign values should use distribution as well as graphology.

Evidence: Different distributions of similarly drawn fish/oval/jar classes.

Tests: C1-R. Status: Exploratory.

## Q048

**evaluation criterion**. Source: L12, 0:37:10–0:37:30.

A candidate language should not be imposed through a prior preference.

Evidence: Comparison with unsuccessful aspirational readings.

Tests: C1-R. Status: Exploratory.

## Q049

**evaluation criterion**. Source: L12, 0:41:42–0:42:25.

Readings should not require ad hoc explanations for implausible content.

Evidence: Examples of bizarre readings excused as mystical formulas.

Tests: C1-R. Status: Exploratory.

## Q050

**evaluation criterion**. Source: L12, 0:46:18–0:46:40.

Claims to read virtually every sign and every cited inscription deserve scrutiny.

Evidence: Incomplete coverage persists in established decipherments.

Tests: C1-R. Status: Exploratory.

## Q051

**method**. Source: B24, Foreword, PDF pp. 4; earlier values restated and withdrawn in PDF pp. 55–66.

He reports developing metrological field analysis in the 1990s and arguing for Indo-Aryan by 2010, before his later expanded value system.

Evidence: The 2024 author retrospective dates the field analysis and initial language argument; it describes a 2014 monograph and later methodological failure. This is his retrospective chronology, rather than a new direct collation of the 2010 witness.

Tests: C1-R. Status: Exploratory.

## Q052

**method**. Source: B24, Second-edition foreword, PDF pp. 7–8.

He describes the second edition as nearly identical to the first, with expanded fish/M-field discussion, one corrected sign and affected readings, and an added KARAPALA ligature.

Evidence: The second-edition foreword identifies these changes; first-edition full text is unavailable for a line-by-line collation.

Tests: V1, C1-R. Status: Exploratory.

## Q053

**method**. Source: B-letter, Introductory letter hosted by Jeff Lindsay, PDF p. 5.

The undated letter says the later ICIT cross-check invalidated a couple of previously proposed sign values.

Evidence: The separate letter’s PDF p. 5 gives the author’s account; Lecture 7 and the second-edition manuscript foreword each report one withdrawn value.

Tests: V1. Status: Exploratory.

Source wording: “invalidated only a couple of previously-proposed sign values”

## Q054

**historical, linguistic or archaeological**. Source: L02, 0:23:46–0:27:00.

He presents Indo-Aryan speakers as a candidate for Indus and separates it from politically motivated arguments.

Evidence: Historical overview; no independent excavation-language join.

Tests: . Status: Exploratory.

## Q071

**sign value (phonetic or logographic)**. Source: B24, Table 18, PDF pp. 80.

He assigns pala/paala/bala to the Table 18 entry labelled PAla.

Evidence: The Table 18 drawing/value list and the author’s derivation passages are distinguished in value_derivation_evidence. The lexical scorer uses manuscript roles; agreed-only changes eligibility.

Tests: L1, P-C, P-L, S2-A, S2-L, PR-L, PR-H, PR-D. Status: Exploratory.

## Q072

**sign value (phonetic or logographic)**. Source: B24, Table 18, PDF pp. 80.

Table 18 labels the bow-alone drawing DAna1, with proposed dhana/dhāna/dāna values; the section heading reverses the DAna1/DAna2 labels.

Evidence: The Table 18 drawing/value list and the author’s derivation passages are distinguished in value_derivation_evidence. The lexical scorer uses manuscript roles; agreed-only changes eligibility.

Tests: L1, P-C, P-L, S2-A, S2-L, PR-L, PR-H, PR-D. Status: Exploratory.

Derivation: Bow/wealth near-homonym and numeral/asset contexts; later section labels reverse DAna1/DAna2. Manuscript pages 45, 46, 47, 55, 132, 134.
